          Request:          Request for approval of a site plan for a new charter school and approval of
                            a plat amendment to create two lots from one existing lot.


SUMMARY
This application is a request for approval of a site plan and plat amendment located at approximately
12197 S. 300 East (see Exhibit C). At the time of the writing at this staff report, the site was currently
zoned A5 with a pending request to rezone it to CN. On November 12, the Planning Commission
submitted a unanimous negative recommendation for the rezone application. The City Council public
hearing for the rezone is scheduled for their November 17, 2020 meeting. Should that rezone be
approved at that meeting, the site would then be zoned CN and the proposed plat amendment could create
two lots bisected by a new road. The northern lot where the proposed charter school would be located
would be approximately 6.11 acres (Site) and the southern lot would be approximately 2.64 acres. No
development is proposed on the southern lot at this time. A new road, 12200 South, would connect from
the terminus of 12230 South on the east and curve north to connect with the existing roundabout on 300
East. The subject of this report is for the site plan for the Site and a plat amendment request. If the
rezone is not approved by the City Council on November 17, then the plat amendment would have to be
withdrawn by the applicant as the property cannot be subdivided as currently proposed. The proposed
charter school is for grades K-12 and is anticipated to have a maximum enrollment of 1,000 students.




Beehive Academy                                                                      Apps. # SPR-1005-2020 and
Site Plan and Plat Amendment Request                   1                                      SUBD-1017-2020
BACKGROUND
The subject property has historically been utilized as agricultural or farming land since at least 1978 when
the City incorporated. As noted above, there is a concurrent application to rezone the property from A5 to
CN.


ANALYSIS
General Plan and Zoning. The Land Use Map of the General Plan identifies the property as
Neighborhood Commercial (see Exhibit D). This category is characterized as follows:




The property is currently assigned the A5 zoning classification, which allows both residential and
agricultural uses (see Exhibit F). The purpose of the agricultural zones per Draper City Municipal Code
(DCMC) Section 9-8-020 is “to preserve and protect agricultural lands and related activities, to permit
activities normally and necessarily related to agricultural production, and to prohibit land uses that may
undermine continued agricultural activity.” It further states “that the purpose of the A5 zone is to
maintain the status of large tracts of agricultural land by allowing most commercial agricultural uses.
Typical uses include farming, dairy, and cattle production.”

The property is proposed to be assigned the CN zoning classification, supporting small retail, commercial
and office uses (Exhibit E). Per DCMC Section 9-8-020: “The purpose of the CN zone is to provide areas
where convenience buying outlets, having small trade areas, may be established to serve surrounding
residential neighborhoods. This zone is intended to promote a combination of retail and service facilities
which, in character and scale, meet day to day needs of nearby residents.”




Beehive Academy                                                                       Apps. # SPR-1005-2020 and
Site Plan and Plat Amendment Request                 2                                         SUBD-1017-2020
The property is surrounded by a mix of zoning designations. To the east is the CSD-DD (Day Dairy
Commercial Special District) and CC (Community Commercial) zone. The areas to the south are zoned
CC. On the west side, across 300 East, is CR (Regional Commercial) zoning. To the north, the
residential area is zoned RA2 (Residential Agricultural, 20,000 square foot lot minimum).

State Code. Per Title 10 of the Utah Code Annotated Code (UCA), a municipality’s purview in the
establishment of a public school or charter school is limited. UCA Subsection 10-9a-305(7)(a) states that
“a charter school shall be considered a permitted use in all zoning districts within a municipality”.
Although this application is not a rezone application this point is additive to the understanding of the
rights of Charter Schools. Further, UCA Subsection 10-9a-305(2)(a) states “except as provided in
Subsection (3), a school district or charter school is subject to a municipality’s land use ordinances.”
Furthermore UCA Subsection10-9a-305(2)(b) states that “notwithstanding subsection (3), a municipality
may: subject a charter school to standards within each zone pertaining to setback, height, bulk and
massing regulations, off-site parking, curb cut, traffic circulation, and construction staging; and
impose regulations upon the location of a project that are necessary to avoid unreasonable risks to
health or safety, as provided in subsection (3)(f).” UCA Subsection 10-9a-305(3)(a) also states that: “A
municipality may not impose requirements for landscaping, fencing, aesthetic considerations,
construction methods or materials, additional building inspections, municipal building codes, building
use for educational purposes, or the placement or use of temporary classroom facilities on school
property.” Most of this staff review, is therefore, informational only.

Site Plan
Charter School Site Plan Layout. The applicant is proposing the new charter school to be constructed
towards the northern portion of the existing lot, the Site (see Exhibit F). There will be a row of parking
and two access, or pick up and drop off lanes, between the building and northern property line.
Additional parking and access lanes will be located along the eastern side of the lot. An open space area
including a small play area and soccer field will be located to the south of the building.

Landscaping. Per the Utah State Code, the charter school is exempt from any landscaping standards the
City has within its code. However, there will be an area with a small soccer field and landscaping around
the building. The applicant has indicated on their plan that landscaping will take up approximately 1.95
acres, or 31.81%, of the Site.

Parking. The Site plan for the school in Exhibit E shows the proposed parking for the school. Based on
the requirements of the DCMC, the school is required to provide a total of 311 parking stalls. A
minimum of 223 stalls are required for the elementary and middle school and 88 parking stalls are
required for the high school. The applicant has provided 311 stalls on the site. However, a total of 113 of
the required 311 parking stalls have been designated as evening or events stalls for the school. The 113
parking stalls are also the area where the stacking or queuing lanes for the drop off and pick up of
students will occur each school day and therefore unavailable to be used as parking during those times.
This will essentially leave 198 parking stalls for all employees, teachers, visitors, and high school
students who are of driving age. There is a concern by staff that the 198 stalls may not be enough parking
and the DCMC does not have a provision for evening or event stalls in the parking requirements.

DCMC Section 9-3-040 defines a parking space as:

          “PARKING SPACE: An area designated within a building, parking lot, or other parking area for
          the parking or storage of one motor vehicle.”




Beehive Academy                                                                       Apps. # SPR-1005-2020 and
Site Plan and Plat Amendment Request                 3                                         SUBD-1017-2020
In addition, DCMC Section 9-25-030 defines parking to be provided as:

          9-25-030: Parking to be Provided:
          A.     Parking Required: Every land use established under the authority of this title shall have
                 parking as required by this chapter. Each person who establishes any such land use
                 shall provide the required parking. The establishment of a land use shall include any
                 change in use and any increase in the capacity or intensity of an existing use.
          B.     Continuing Obligation to Provide Parking: Provision of parking as required by this
                 chapter shall be a continuing obligation so long as a use continues which requires
                 parking. It shall be unlawful for any building owner, land use operator, or person
                 responsible for providing parking to discontinue or dispense with required parking
                 facilities without providing an alternate parking area which meets the requirements of
                 this chapter.
          C.     Alteration Where Parking Insufficient: A building, structure, or use which lacks sufficient
                 parking as required by this chapter may not be altered, enlarged, or changed unless
                 additional parking for the alteration, enlargement, or change is supplied that meets the
                 requirements of this chapter.

Therefore it is staff’s opinion based on the DCMC, that all required parking must be provided at all times
unless a deviation or shared parking agreement is approved by the Planning Commission. The applicant
has submitted both a parking study and traffic impact study to address the issue (see Exhibits I and J).

In the parking study, it is noted that the 113 stalls used for the stacking or queuing will not be available
for approximately two hours each day. The parking study discusses parking demand rates from the
Institute of Transportation Engineers (ITE) which indicate that a school similar to Beehive Academy
would only need 198 permanent parking stalls to accommodate the anticipated demand. The report finds
that there is sufficient parking at all times for the anticipated demand all day. However, a concern of staff
is the ability of the school to ensure that no parking stalls that are designated for pickup and drop off are
not utilized at all during the day. One car parked in the area could significantly impact the traffic
circulation an area that a City is allowed to regulate per UCA 10-9a-305(2)(b), of the site during either
drop off or pick up. The applicant has noted that it will be something that the school will monitor and
enforce.

DCMC Section 9-25-090(A) and Table 9-25-100 requires the below parking ranges for schools. The
proposed 311 stalls complies with the parking minimum and maximum standard found in the DCMC.
However, as noted above, the 198 stalls recommended falls short of the minimum and maximum
requirements of the DCMC and the natural deviation of 10 percent as outlined in the DCMC. For that
reason, the applicant has requested a parking deviation from the Planning Commission to exceed the
natural deviation of 10 percent.

Planning staff has reviewed the study and found that the report was prepared by a licensed professional
engineer who is specifically trained in traffic engineering. While staff has some concerns with the
findings of the report as noted above, staff would concur that it was prepared by an appropriate
professional who specializes in the field of engineering. After review of the parking study by city staff, it
has been determined that the appropriate number of parking stalls required for the school should be 311
parking stalls.

The Planning Commission has the authority to approve a deviation of parking requirements per DCMC 9-
25-090(C)(4)(a). While there are no specific deviations for school uses, staff would suggest that
subsection (a) is utilized as it is for commercial, retail, office and mixed use developments which is more
like a school rather than residential development. Any deviation authorized by the Planning Commission
would allow the 198 parking stalls to be the required parking instead of the 311 required by the DCMC.
Those findings are as follows:

4.        Findings Required: The planning commission may approve a deviation from strict compliance for
          the number of parking spaces required or the calculation used only after making the findings of
          this subsection:
          a.      For commercial, retail, office, and mixed use developments, the planning commission
                  must find that:
                  (1)      Adequate parking will be provided;
                  (2)      The total number of spaces that would otherwise be required for each individual
                           establishment in the development is overly burdensome for the site specific
                           factors of the application;
                  (3)      Estimated trade offs between businesses which are open when others are closed
                           will not overburden the parking provided;
                  (4)      There is an adequate availability of approved on street or shared parking for all
                           associated uses;

                    (5)       Site or use specific conditions or factors do not provide for compliance with the
                              parking calculation used or parking requirements outlined in Section 9-25-100,
                              Table 9-25-1 of this chapter;
                    (6)       Transportation alternatives, including proximity to transit stations, provide
                              adequate transportation to offset any reduction in parking; and

Staff has included findings for approval or denial and potential motions for the reduction in parking.

Traffic Impact Study. The applicant has provided an updated traffic impact study (TIS), as found in
Exhibit J of this report. The study contains existing and future level of service conditions for current and
future developments, key findings and recommendations along with various mitigation measures.

The TIS indicates, as the city is well aware, that there are significant delays and congestion in the area of
12300 South and 300 East. In order to add the project traffic to the public roads, and continue to have
functioning streets, several of the required mitigation projects identified in the TIS must be constructed
prior to the opening of the school. These improvements are located at 12300 South and 300 East, 12300
South and 450 East, and 700 East and 12200 South.

Traffic is measured by the level of service (LOS) measured by counting vehicles or timing delays and
backups. Current traffic engineering principles state that the typical threshold for acceptable traffic is for
a level of service D, where LOS A is nearly no traffic or delays and LOS F is unacceptable delays.

In an effort to address the deficiencies to accommodate the project traffic, the city, Utah Department of
Transportation (UDOT), and the applicant will work together to construct the necessary improvements. It
is important to note that State law does not allow the applicant to address existing traffic deficiencies per
UCA 10-9a-305(3)(b). Therefore each party listed below will commit to fund and construct the necessary
traffic mitigation projects before the opening of the school:

         UDOT will commit to construct a traffic signal at 450 East and 12300 South
         The applicant will construct the connection of 12200 South through the site and add turning lanes
          on both 700 East and 12200 South. Please note that 12200 South is shown on the city’s master
          transportation plan and is a minor collector street providing additional east-west vehicle travel
          capacity.
         The projects that the city will commit to in order to mitigate existing deficiencies are adding
          turning lanes on 300 East at 12300 South, widening 12200 South from Day Dairy to 700 East,
          and modifying existing intersection configuration at 12300 South and 300 East to add left turn
          lanes.

Per the updated traffic impact study, the resulting LOS in the area will be LOS D or better once the city,
UDOT, and the applicant complete the offsite projects. LOS D still reflects delays, but will function at an
improved level of congestion than exists today after the addition of the project traffic.

Emergency Vehicle Access. The Utah State Fire Marshal’s Office does have jurisdiction over the school,
however, the Draper City Fire Department is the responding agency and has provided comments with
regards to Emergency Vehicle Access. The Utah State Fire Marshal’s Office reviews and inspects all life
safety systems during construction and enforces any requirements of the Draper City Fire Department.




Beehive Academy                                                                            Apps. # SPR-1005-2020 and
Site Plan and Plat Amendment Request                      6                                         SUBD-1017-2020
The access and configuration along the north side of the building is concerning. There is a notation on the
site plan that the 300 East entrance is an emergency access only, but it is not clear how this will be only
used for emergencies. The site plan shows lane configurations with a right or left turn exit along with a
stop sign.

Another concern with the north side of the site plan is that there are two lanes of stacking vehicles which
could block emergency access to the site if an emergency was to occur during the drop off or pick up
hours. There should be a dedicated fire lane that is always left unobstructed. The final concern with the
north side is related to the previous concern as the fire riser is on the north side of the building and there
would be no access to that fire riser if there are cars stacked or parked in that area.

Along the east side of the building, the stacking presents a similar concern as noted above. The site plan
shows both a drop off and pick up lane with a bypass lane. Similar to the north side of the building, the
east side bypass lane needs to be left unobstructed at all times so that emergency vehicles can easily have
access to the building or nearby playground and soccer field if needed

Architecture. The City has the ability to look at certain aspects of architecture per Utah State Code.
Aesthetics and materials may not be considered but height and setbacks may. The proposed building does
meet the setbacks required for either the A5 or the CN zoning districts. The structure is two stories tall
and the plans note the building will be 35 feet tall, meeting the maximum height requirement of 35 feet in
either zone. Staff would typically review proposed structures, other than residential, in accordance with
Section 9-22 (Design Standards and Guidelines) of the DCMC. However, that Section is not applicable.

Lighting. No lighting plan has been provided, nor is the applicant required to provide a plan as per Utah
State Code. However, a condition of approval has been included for the school to comply with the City’s
illumination standards governing the spillover of light from the site to adjacent properties. This condition
is to ensure there is not light pollution on adjacent properties per DCMC Section 9-20-080.

Fencing. The applicant is not required to meet the City’s fencing standards. A new concrete panel fence
is proposed along the north property line and the existing concrete and vinyl fence along the east property
line will remain. The open space or playground area will be surrounded by black PVC coated chain link
fence.

Plat Amendment
The subject property contains approximately 9.8 acres. The intent is to create two lots out of the 9.8 acres
(see Exhibit H). The proposed subdivision will be possible should the City Council decide to rezone the
property to CN. With the current zoning of A5, it would be not be possible to subdivide as each lot
(minus any road right-of-way) must be at least five acres in size.

Should the rezone be approved, the following is the proposed lot layout. The northern lot, the Site, would
be approximately 6.116 acres in size and would be the site utilized by the proposed charter school and as
described in the site plan analysis above. The southern lot would be approximately 2.643 acres in size
and would be developed with commercial uses. A new public street, called 12200 South, would link the
current terminus of 12230 South and curve to the north and west to connect with the existing roundabout
on 300 East. The road would encompass approximately 1.04 acres.

Should the rezoning not be approved, the proposed charter school would still be on northern portion of
the current lot and the plat amendment application would no longer be possible and would need to be
withdrawn. The school would have the same layout; however, the total size of the school lot would total
approximately 8.75 acres. This would require the applicant to utilize both the area north and south of the
proposed road as the school. The area to the south could be developed at a future date, but would be part



Beehive Academy                                                                          Apps. # SPR-1005-2020 and
Site Plan and Plat Amendment Request                   7                                          SUBD-1017-2020
of the school property and therefore owned by the school. The public street would then need to be created
by another means in order to dedicate it as public right-of-way to the city and install the road and all
associated improvements. The school would have control of the area south of the road and could utilize it
as part of the school or another rezone application could be submitted in the future to rezone the property.
However, DCMC Section 9-5-060(H) would preclude another rezone application being submitted for a
period of one year after a denial. The only exception is that the Planning Commission or City Council
has the authority to initiate a rezone application within that one year period.


Criteria for Approval. The criteria for review and potential approval of a commercial site plan request is
found in Sections 9-5-090(E) of the DCMC. This section depicts the standard of review for such requests
as:

E.        Standards for Approval. The following standards shall apply to the approval of a site plan.

          1.        The entire site shall be developed at one time unless a phased development plan is
                    approved. This is not applicable for charter schools under state law.

          2.        A site plan shall conform to applicable standards set forth in this title, including but not
                    limited to, building heights, setbacks, access points, parking, landscaping, and building
                    materials. Landscaping and building materials are not applicable for charter schools
                    under state law. All other standards can be considered.

          3.        The proposed development plans meet the intent, goals, and objectives of the general
                    plan and the purpose of the zone district in which the site is located. This is not
                    applicable for charter schools under state law. Charter schools are considered a
                    permitted use regardless of the zone and the general plan.

          4.        The public facilities and services in the area are adequate to support the subject
                    development, as required by engineering standards and specifications.

          5.        The proposed development plans comply with the engineering standards found in Titles
                    7, 8, 11, 12, 16, and 18 of this code, including traffic, storm water drainage, and utilities
                    concerns.

The criteria for review and potential approval of a Plat Amendment request is found in Section 17-9-040
of the DCMC. This section depicts the standard of review for such requests as:

          “The Planning Commission may approve the vacation or amendment of a plat by signing an
          amended plat showing the vacation or amendment if the Land Use Authority finds that there is
          good cause for the vacation or amendment; and no public street, right-of-way, or easement has
          been vacated or amended.”

STAFF RECOMMENDATION
Staff recommends that the Planning Commission review the request, receive public comment, and make a
decision based on the findings listed below and the criteria for approval listed within the staff report.

If the Planning Commission decides to approve the request, staff recommends that they include the
following conditions of approval:

1.        This project falls under the jurisdiction of the State Board of Education and shall comply with all
          the requirements throughout the development of the site and the construction of all buildings on
          the site, including permitting of UCA 53E-3-702.
2.        This project falls under the jurisdiction of the State Fire Marshal and shall comply with all the
          requirements throughout the development of the site and the construction of all buildings on the
          site, including permitting of UCA 53E-3-706.
3.        That all requirements of the geotechnical report are satisfied throughout the development of the
          site and the construction of all buildings on the site.
4.        Incorporate emergency vehicle access analysis recommendations and coordinate with the both the
          State Fire Marshal’s Office and the Draper Fire Marshal to develop working plans for emergency
          access during peak drop off and pick up times.
5.        The applicant shall provide a circulation plan for all school functions to the Draper City Fire
          Marshal prior to the opening of the school.
6.        Prior to issuance of a Land Disturbance Permit, the applicant shall provide a commitment (or
          agreement) for each of the following projects, as identified:
          a.        Utah Department of Transportation (UDOT): construction of the traffic signal at
                    450 East and 12300 South;
          b.        Applicant: construction of the connection of 12200 South through the site;
          c.        Applicant: construction of a right turn lane on both 700 East and 12200 South at
                    the intersection;
          d.        City: construction of the left turning lanes on 300 East at 12300 South; and
          e.        City: construction of the widening of 12200 South from Day Dairy to 700 East.
7.        Obtain a Land Disturbance Permit prior to construction activities onsite.
8.        An encroachment permit is required for any work in a public right-of-way.
9.        Verify drainage report includes the full site development conditions, if the full 8.75 acre site is
          required due to zoning, and add the final site plan layout, including site phasing plan, to report.
10        On site lighting should comply with standards found in Section 9-20-070 and 9-20-080(A)
          (Illumination Standards) of the Draper City Municipal Code.

Plat Amendment
Sample Motion for Approval – I move that we approve the Plat Amendment, as requested by Pace CM,
representing the Rasmussen Limited Family Partnership, application SUBD-1017-2020, based on the
findings and subject to the conditions listed in the staff report dated November 10, 2020.

Sample Motion for Denial – I move that we deny the Plat Amendment, as requested by Pace CM,
representing the Rasmussen Limited Family Partnership, application SUBD-1017-2020, based on the
findings and subject to the conditions listed in the staff report dated November 10, 2020.




Beehive Academy                                                                        Apps. # SPR-1005-2020 and
Site Plan and Plat Amendment Request                  11                                        SUBD-1017-2020
                                     EXHIBIT A
                            DEPARTMENT/DIVISION COMMENTS

                  REVIEWS ARE NOT MEANT TO BE AN ALL INCLUSIVE LIST OF
                           POSSIBLE COMMENTS OR CONDITIONS.

Planning Division Review.
1.     The parking study has shown that a reduced number of parking stalls is appropriate for the
       proposed charter school based on data of other charter schools. The DCMC does not have a
       provision to base parking on data from other schools. The Planning Commission can authorize a
       deviation of parking.
2.     Planning staff has considered the following standards when reviewing the proposed school.
       Those are: parking, circulations, building setbacks and building height. Landscaping, buffering
       and fencing standard cannot be imposed by a municipality.

Engineering and Public Works Divisions Review.
1.     Stormwater Maintenance Plan and Agreement are now required for every private storm drainage
       system, per DCMC 16-2-170. Submission of the maintenance plan is now required prior to site
       approval. The maintenance agreement is a condition of approval; it is required to be recorded
       and returned to the city prior to issuance of the Land Disturbance Permit for construction of the
       site.
2.     The developer is to provide a drainage report including detention, orifice, and pipe sizing
       calculations per DCMC 17-3-030(C)(9) and 17-3-030(F). The drainage report is to meet the
       requirement of the City of Draper Drainage Design Criteria available online. Consider
       implementing LID (low-impact development) BMP’s in the overall drainage design.
3.     Provide service letters from South Valley Sewer District (SVSD) and WaterPro per DCMC 17-
       3-030(I) and DCMC 9-5-090(D)(1)(d)(3)(E).
4.     Once site plan has been approved, a Land Disturbance Permit is required prior to construction
       activities onsite. Permit is obtained through the Engineering Division and is required prior to
       Building Permit issuance. This is an informational comment that will remain through the
       review process.
5.     Any work in the public right-of-way will require an Encroachment Permit obtained through the
       Engineering Division. This is an informational comment that will remain through the review
       process.
6.     UPDATED TIS RECEIVED. Comments provided in meetings and email coordination.
       Resolution to city’s concerns ongoing and includes meetings and coordination with UDOT.
7.     Drainage report provides NOAA precipitation for the area of approximately one inch of rainfall
       depth for the three-hour, 10-year storm. Draper City criteria, in the city’s drainage design guide
       requires 0.93 inches depth for the three-hour, 10-year storm. Applicant may choose to keep the
       conservative storm depth.
8.     In the introduction of the drainage report, it references an incorrect address for the project
       location. In the appendix, the drainage basin and pipeline flow calculations shows an incorrect
       title and general information.
9.     In the Hydrology Parameters section of the report, it states, “In the event a storm surcharges the
       system….” Provide a determination of what storm will surcharge the system. Similarly,
       provide a narrative of the 100-year storm event onsite.
10.    Identify the water quality component of the site storm drainage design in the drainage report.
11.    Pipe 1 is the SD pipeline from the basin to the city’s system. This pipe requires the “n” value to
       be 0.015 for RCP per the drainage design guide. Verify onsite “n” values incorporate the minor
       losses, per the drainage design requirement.
12.     Existing irrigation ditch enters the site at the northeast corner of the site. Provide documentation
        that the ditch can be abandoned, by the irrigation company / water master, or continue ditch
        through site to the tail water discharge location. Verify this site is the only water user on the
        ditch. Response comment indicates East Jordan Canal Company no longer requires this ditch
        and there are no active users of this ditch. This comment is for information only, no additional
        action required.
13.     Raised median required at ingress (eastern most access) to prevent left-hand turn movements
        into the site from 12230 South.

Building Division Review.
No comments provided.

Addressing/GIS Review.
No comments provided.

Draper City Fire Review.
This project falls under the jurisdiction of the Utah State Fire Marshal’s Office however; Draper City Fire
Department is the responding agency and has made some comments below.

1.      Fire Department Access is required. An unobstructed minimum road width of 26 feet exclusive
        of the shoulders and a minimum height of 13’6” inches shall be required. The road must be
        designed and maintained to support the imposed loads of emergency apparatus. The surface shall
        be able to provide all weather driving capabilities. The road shall have an inside turning radius of
        28 feet. There shall be a maximum grade of 10%. Grades may be checked prior to building
        permits being issued.
        A.       There are two access points required that are half the diagonal distance apart of the
                 overall property. This seems to meet this requirement. The parking study and the site
                 plan do not match which layout is correct the site plan with the cul-de-sac and entry or
                 the one without.
        B.       How are you controlling the one way entry/exit?
        C.       On the North drive, how would the fire department access this entry/exit if that area is
                 stacked with cars?
        D.       If the fire riser is on the North side of the building, with the stacking we would have no
                 access to the fire department connection.
2.      Hydrants and Site Access. All hydrants and a form of acceptable temporary Fire Department
        Access to the site shall be installed and APPROVED by the Fire Department prior to the issuance
        of any Building Permits. If at any time during the building phase any of the hydrants or
        temporary Fire Department Access becomes non-compliant any and all permits could be revoked.
        A.       The required fire flow for this project if it is type VB construction is 2875gpm that is with
                 a 50% reduction in fire flow due to fire sprinklers
        B.       With the reduction for fire sprinklers you only need 3 fire hydrants spaced at 300 feet and
                 there needs to be a fire hydrant within 100 feet of the fire department connection.
3.      No combustible construction shall be allowed prior to hydrant installation and testing by water
        purveyor. All hydrants must be operational prior to any combustible elements being received or
        delivered on building site.
4.      Fire Sprinklers Required. A deferred submittal for a NFPA 13 fire sprinkler shop drawings are to
        be sent via email to: Don Buckley at don.buckley@draper.ut.us. A complete set of plans, with
        manufacturer cut sheets, and hydraulic calculations. Plans must be stamped by a NICET level III
        or better in Auto Sprinkler Layout. (There needs to be a hydrant with-in a 100 feet of the FDC.)
        FDC is required to have KNOX Locking Caps. ALL FIRE PROTECTION PLANS REQUIRE
     3rd PARTY REVIEW PRIOR TO BE SUBMITTED TO THE DRAPER FIRE DEPARTMENT
     AND THE STATE FIRE MARSHALS OFFICE.
5.   Post Indicator Valve with Tamper May Be Required. If there is no designated fire riser room
     with a direct access door from the outside. There shall be either a wall mounted P.I.V (OS&Y) or
     a typical P.I.V placed a minimum distance of 40 feet from the building with a tamper switch.
6.   Fire Alarm Required. A deferred submittal for an NFPA 72 fire alarm shop drawings are to be
     sent via email to the following to: Don Buckley at don.buckley@draper.ut.us. A complete set of
     plans, with manufacturer cut sheets, and battery calculations. Plans must be stamped by a NICET
     level III or better in Fire Alarm Systems. FDC is required to have KNOX Locking Caps. ALL
     FIRE PROTECTION PLANS REQUIRE 3rd PARTY REVIEW PRIOR TO BE SUBMITTED
     TO THE DRAPER FIRE DEPARTMENT AND THE STATE FIRE MARSHALS OFFICE.
7.   Knox Boxes Required. Fire Department “Knox Brand” lock box to be mounted to exterior walls,
     near the main entrance and/or nearest the door serving the exterior access to the fire sprinkler
     riser room (at a height of 5 feet to the top of the box). Lock box purchase can be arranged by the
     General Contractor.

PUBLIC COMMENTS
Maryann Pickering

From:                            Bob Millerberg <bobmillerberg@gmail.com>
Sent:                            Wednesday, November 4, 2020 1:24 PM
To:                              Maryann Pickering
Subject:                         proposed rezone of 300 E 12200 S



Dear MaryAnn Pickering,

My name is Bob Millerberg and I reside at 491 E 12000 S Draper. We have lived in our home since 2002.
When we moved to our home it was on a quiet deadend street. Since that time Draper has grown and now we
have to wait for traffic to clear before we can get onto 12000 S when leaving our home.

I know and understand that Draper has an open road policy, however more and more traffic is being thrust upon
our residential street due to lack of planning to protect neighborhoods. Draper's thirst for commercial property
and growth are endangering the neighborhoods that once made the community strong.

I strongly oppose the proposed rezone of the property located on 300 E and approximately 12200 S. without
measures being taken for traffic concerns for the residence of these neighborhoods. As it sits right now 12300 S.
is not adequately handling the traffic on it. Traffic is constantly diverting to the residential streets of 12000 S
and 12100 S. This has become a major safety concern. With the anticipation of 800 additional morning trips,
and 600 additional evening trips the school would bring, combined with the already heavy traffic congestion,
this is an accident waiting to happen. As we have seen with Juan Diego, school traffic is the worst as people are
always late and speeding through our neighborhoods.

In summary this is simply too much traffic for the residential streets in the area. Safety concerns for the
residents need to be accounted for. Bringing this much traffic to a neighborhood should not be allowed and this
project needs to be put on hold until a solution the whole community can live with is reached.

I would also request to be added to any correspondence in regard to said property.

Thanks for your consideration.




                                                         1
Maryann Pickering

From:                           Scorzato, Kelly <Kelly.Scorzato@va.gov>
Sent:                           Wednesday, November 4, 2020 4:32 PM
To:                             Andrew Adams; Mary Squire; Craig Hawker; Kent Player; John Van Hoff; Gary Ogden;
                                Lisa Fowler; delainatonks@gmail.com; Tab Bingham; Maryann Pickering

Subject:                        Oppose the Forced Connection of 12230 South to 3rd East (thru Rasmussen Farms) -
                                Rasmussen Family Commercial ZA


RE: Oppose the Forced Connection of 12230 South to 3rd East (thru Rasmussen Farms) - Rasmussen
Family Commercial ZA

   -    If Draper City needs an emergency exit – I like the idea of the crash gate on 12200. That makes sense.
   -    Increasing the traffic on 3rd E does not make good civil servant judgement. (it creates a more unsafe
        environment)

The Street Plan/Master Plan is outdated and needs to be updated to address the implications of all the high
density that has been built in the last 5 years!
Connecting 12230 thru to 3rd East – a 10 Year Old Plan, that did not address the most recent approved/built
high density housing – is bad for Draper City. Draper City government needs to take the lead on prioritizing
safer streets. Years ago, cities made roads work more efficiently for cars, but made them more dangerous for
people, Traffic safety improves livability, walkability, and public health. Prioritizing safety uses a people-first
approach to eliminate deaths and serious injuries.

Streets should be designed to minimize through traffic.
     My children go to school at Juan Diego.
     Children walk to and from school. It is already extremely dangerous for them. The additional traffic
       would only compound the problem.
     The public school bus pick-up is on 3rd East. More traffic would make this dangerous for the children.
     Many people from Draper Village walk their dogs on 3rd E.
     Many children walk on 3rd E to and from school activities
     When Charter Schools is approved it will add more traffic.
     This small residential road – 3rd E cannot handle the burdensome traffic created by the connection of
       12230 S to 3rd East (thru Rasmussen Farms.)

I am always informing the city about the short-cut traffic on 121st street and always requesting our speed
bumps be repaired. I have had many short-cut drivers try to pass me on the left side. On one occasion I was
driving on 3rd East, and started to go slowly, because there was a high school track team running, I had
vehicle try dangerously pass me on the road, because I was going to slow and almost hit some of the track
team. These drivers have no concept of pedestrian safety.
If Draper City needs an emergency exit – I like the idea of the crash gate on 12200. That makes sense.
Increasing the traffic on 3rd E does not make good civil servant judgement. (it creates a more unsafe
environment)

From:                              Sharon Lisk <sharonlisk@gmail.com>
Sent:                              Wednesday, November 4, 2020 8:18 PM
To:                                Maryann Pickering
Subject:                           Concerns with Rezoning project in Draper (3rd E & 12200 S)



Hello,
We are writing to record and voice our concerns about the possible rezoning of the 10 acres just north of the
McDonalds and Chick-fil-A in Draper.
We feel that the building of a 1000-student school would overburden the area with traffic and congestion. 3rd
East is already extremely busy with school traffic, townhouse/apartment and neighborhood traffic, in addition to
all of the “cut-through “ traffic from 12300 headed toward State street.
Additionally, we are especially concerned about the possibility of adding a connector of 12200 to the round-
about on 3rd east. That round-about is already extremely congested many times a day- lunchtime, after school,
after work hours.
Connecting 12230 to 3rd is a reckless decision.

Why Connecting 12230 thru to 3rd East is Dangerous for the Community
   This street plan/master plan road connection is detrimental to the safety and welfare of our neighborhoods &
      community.
   May have been a logical concept 10 years ago – but that did not take into account all the recent high density
      residential housing. It is now problematic
   All the high density housing/commercial traffic going west on 12200 S, will go thru the Rasmussen
      Development to use 3rd East as a shortcut - instead of 12300. (5 less lights)
           Parc Day Dairy Apartments12200 S (2013) 228 units + 2 cars
           Victoria Wood Apartments 12200 S (2014) – 42 units + 2 cars
           Day Dairy 12200 S ( future) - will consist of 3 new retail buildings, with several matching townhome
              buildings (substantial traffic) – potentially + 500 plus
           Corner of 12200 S and 7th East (future) - Neighborhood Commercial – + 50 cars
           Customers at the retail stores on 12300 will use this thru street. +100 cars
   3rd East is already hazardous with its current traffic;
           Draper Village, high density housing of 184 units +2 cars
           Draper Post Office on 3rd East - generates substantial area. It was originally on master plan to be built
              further east, but the neighbors did not want the additional traffic, so moved to 3rd E.
           Juan Diego Schools +75 cars
           Draperville Community +50 cars
           Cottage Field Community +5 cars
           Ivory Homes
           include the local residential neighborhoods, and others from 3 rd E that use this street as a shortcut to
              State Street.
           3rd E. there is often a grid back-up of several cars, at the roundabout that makes it unsafe.

Kids walk to school on 3rd East, pedestrians walk their dogs, there are cyclists, school bus picks up/drops school kids, and
many local residents have equestrian property.

Thank you for your consideration of our and our neighbors’ concerns about this project being approved.

Sharon and Andy Lisk
419 E 12100 S

       to 3rd E no longer makes sense, since this residential street is so small and can barely handle current traffic. This
       is a separate zoning proposal and should not be forced on Rasmussen Farms.

Mitigation for Not Connecting 12230 to 3rd E.
     UDOT study of 450 East/12300 South intersections warrants a signal. This will address the traffic needs of
        12230/12200 S.

Academy, north of McDonald’s and Chick-Fil-A. We are long time residents of Draper and reside on 300 East,
approximately 100 yards down the street from the proposed site. We are committed to this area and its future
development. While we understand that the lot will not remain an agricultural field forever, we do not believe
that another K-12 school in this area is the right fit. It is probably no surprise that our primary concerns are
traffic and safety.

The Skaggs Catholic Complex and the K-12 schools already here, plus the existing commercial entities on 300
east already place a huge strain on the intersection of 300 East and 12300 S. As stated in the traffic study
done by Hales Engineering in conjunction with the proposal (pg. ii), this intersection already operates at a
failing level of service, even without the added stress of the projected 800 additional morning trips, and 600
additional afternoon trips that the new proposed school would bring.

We have several major concerns with this traffic study overall. The first is that it was done on a Wednesday,
which would not necessarily give an accurate representation of the afternoon traffic burden because Juan
Diego Catholic High school has its early release day on Wednesdays. It says some data was collected on
Thursday, but does not specify what. We think that this is an issue that needs attention and
clarification. Additionally, it is relying on data collected over a year ago. It also does not take into account the
impact of the expansion already in progress at the Parc at Day Dairy, nor does it consider the impact of
whatever commercial development will eventually go into place if the lot under consideration is
subdivided. Clearly, the southern 3+ acre portion that would remain zoned Neighborhood Commercial will add
even MORE stress once it is built out, but there is no mention of this in their study. Where would the ingress
and egress for that portion of land be, and how will it impact the flow from the proposed school? The impact of
all of this development would surely amount to a larger growth rate than 1% per year as predicted in their
study. A third frustration is the inaccuracy of the wait times. The study lists a delay of less than or equal to 80
seconds at the 300 East/12300 S intersection. As residents who daily drive that intersection, we can attest that
it is very common to wait 2, and sometimes 3, turn signals to be able to turn left onto 12300 S, or left onto 300
east from 12300 S. It easily represents a delay of longer than 80 seconds at various times during the day even
before the added traffic flow from the proposed development.

As we are sure you are familiar, the proposed mitigations to alleviate this failing intersection involve adding a
left turn lane and a longer right turn pocket on 300 east, widening of 12300 South, and extending the left turn
wait lane at on 12300 S, in addition to adding a turn signal at 450 E and 12300 S. These are large
modifications that would take a long time and a lot of money to implement. Where is the funding going to come
from to make them happen? When will the city prioritize the problem enough to address the issue? We have
come to planning commission and city council hearings for years trying to bring the problem to the forefront
                                                         1
when other developments along our street have been in progress, and nothing has ever been done to fix
it. How will you make 300 east safe enough and efficient enough for TWO K-12 schools? Let alone two
high schools worth of teenage drivers filling it at the same times each day? It is very frustrating to see in the
traffic study that the end goal, even with these costly and intensive mitigations in place, is still a level of traffic
that is “approaching unstable”. That’s the end goal. A letter grade of D; acceptable flow approaching
unstable. How can this be right for our neighborhood? And that’s just for the 12300 S/300 East
intersection. The retail access for McDonald’s/Chick-Fil-A and the Holiday Oil/Auto Zone on 300 East will still
have a letter grade of F, with forced flows and unstable delays. That is an area that is already a mess on a
daily basis.

Another aspect of concern is the direct egress onto 300 east from the school property. In the traffic study (pg.
12) it states that “It is anticipated that this access will be closed during the morning drop off time.” This is a
very vague promise and one that could be easily skirted later on. It is highly reminiscent of the promise made
by the Skaggs Catholic Center that they would never punch through to 300 east on the south portion of their
property. We all know that was an empty promise, the breaking of which brought a large amount of additional
school traffic to 300 East; a street that is defined in the Draper City Master Transportation Plan as a “minor
collector” roadway. The amount of traffic sustained by this portion of 300 east could hardly be deemed minor,
and the proposed development would exponentially increase it. The Transportation Master Plan did not intend
to ever allow this much traffic on our street, and the proposed school should not be allowed to be built.

A final issue is safety. The speed limit on our street is routinely ignored, and the area in general isn’t as safe
for pedestrians as it could be. For example, we have a school bus stop for our elementary aged children at 300
East and 12090 S. At least twice a month and sometimes once a week, we witness cars ignoring the flashing
red lights and stop sign to blow past the school bus at its stop. It poses a major hazard to our kids who have to
cross the street to get on and off the bus. The additional foot traffic that a second school will bring needs to be
considered (though we recognize that the charter school will not run buses), and we need to figure out what we
can do to get cars to slow down and watch for kids. We already have a lot of foot traffic from the children who
attend the schools at Juan Diego, and the safe crossing points are spread too far apart on 300 east.

We appreciate your time and consideration of our perspective. Please do whatever is in your power to deny
the zone change and site plan approval, this just isn’t the right fit for our area.

Sincerely,
Jeff and Lisa Hansen
12057 S 300 E
Draper, UT 84020

many, many people access via 300 East. I live on 300 East, across from the newer Draper
Village apartment complex (which basically doubled the traffic here). The road is already
overcrowded at key times and unsafe generally because of the speed at which people drive
on the straight road. Every day that Juan Diego School is in session, there are cars parked
on 11950 South because Juan Diego cannot accommodate the number of cars in their
parking lot. There used to be a problem with people parking on 300 East, but after years of
fighting for them, the City finally installed No Parking signs in certain areas of 300 East. In
addition to the general traffic congestion nightmare that another school will add (a school
with a larger student body than Juan Diego has), there will also most likely be another
parking issue. The proposed plan only shows a 1:4 ratio for their high school students.
None of the Beehive Academy's current student body is from this neighborhood so it is safe
to assume that every student will need to drive or be driven there. It seems likely that they
have not allotted enough parking. It would be very hazardous, but the only options, for
parking to spill over onto either 300 East or 12230 South. A formerly proposed Richmond
American development was denied for exactly this reason, parking shortage.
The traffic study suggests some possible helps to ease congestion, but they are fairly major
construction projects and won't be high priority. We have been asking for help with traffic
on 300 East ever since Juan Diego opened the south entrance (that was closed off when I
built my home on 300 East). We have never received any help for speeding by way of
signage, speed tables, etc. I wish someone with some real common sense would take a look
at a map showing the current developments within 1 mile of the proposed school and new
commercial project. There are already several multi-family housing developments. Chick-fil-
A is easily one of the busiest fast food places in all of Draper. 12300 South is the main
artery of Draper and wildly overcrowded. It often takes me multiple light cycles to be able to
turn left onto 300 East if I am heading east on 12300 South. Dozens of cars a day access
Draper Peaks shopping center by way of 300 East. People prefer to use that back way
instead of 12300 South. Dozens of cars access the Post Office by way of 300 East each day.
I understand that the City has plans to put a light on 12300 South at the Parc complex
entrance. That is a fine idea, but why would anyone use that congested street when they
can take residential back roads and get there faster and with no lights? The answer is, they
won't. If an access to the school and new commercial projects is created off of the 300 East
roundabout, that will become the PRIMARY access regardless of where the main entrance
signs are posted. With the addition of multi-family residential developments on 700 East
and 12200 South, the traffic on 700 East has also become overwhelming at peak times.
This many cars and people cannot and should not try to fit in such a small area.

street as 12200 would be punched through and take traffic pressure from anything developed to the south; I was also told
that a Park was going in where the Post Office now sits; I was also told that 300 E. would not be completed... We now
experience significantly more traffic on our street; merging onto 700 E, or 300 E. from either end of 12100 at certain times
of the day is very frustrating as they are so busy with heavy traffic. I know I sound bitter, and I am. My wife and I bought
our home partly based on what we were told by the City. Our dream house / neighborhood is not what we expected. We
moved to our current home from the Sandy area as we wanted to be in a more rural area with more space. We are now
in an area with significantly more traffic congestion and more development that our prior Sandy location. Traffic on the
freeway exit / entrance is also very frustrating at times. Adding developments that will increase the traffic will only make
things worse. I am also very concerned that with the increase in high density housing units (allowed recently) there is a
significant safety issue for children as well as all others in our neighborhood. Crime, neighborhood complaints and
concerns about safety and security of property have increased. These recent changes allowing more housing units, and
increased commercial development are not making Draper a better place to live, we are becoming just another City more
concerned about tax revenue than quality of life for its citizens. Draper council should represent its citizen constituents
not developers.
Please do not allow this rapid, ill planned growth to continue.
Gary Tsujimoto

Subject:                         Beehive School Plans



Maryann,

I wanted to take a minute to voice serious concern over the plans to put a new K-12 school on the open lot on
300 E. I live on 12000 S which feeds off of 300 E. Since the Juan Diego school opened the egress onto 300 E
we have been living with daily and weekly safety concerns and massive traffic issues on 300 E.

Almost daily I had to pull my children off of the street when they are walking (there are not sidewalks the entire
length of the street) to protect them from cars (mostly Juan Diego students and parents who use 12000 S as
access to 700 E) who speed through our neighborhood usually going 10-15mph over the posted speed limit.
Also in the mornings and when school lets out the congestion on 300 E is untenable and delays our commutes
considerably.

Also, we already have problems with the existing business traffic (Chick-fil-a and Holiday Oil) on 300 E that
has led to accidents and serious congestion. Also, the recent move to make 700 E more of a business district has
made traffic problems increase as traffic is diverted down these side roads to avoid congestion on 123000 S.

The idea of adding another K-12 school within only a few hundred yards of each other is honestly comical. The
traffic is already a massive issue, and without serious drastic (expensive) changes to the entire areas traffic flow
there is no way adding a school of this size will not cause serious safety issues. Not to mention, changes of the
scope that would be required would likely have other unintended consequences.

Please consider that your team may not be aware of the real seriousness of the traffic. Please visit when school
is already being let in and out, or during lunch/dinner hours when business is flowing. Our neighborhood has
already borne the burden of the decisions made to allow Juan Diego access to 300 E, the changing of zoning on
700 E and the multiple high density developments that surround us. This new K-12 school is just one step too
far.

From:                           Rebecca Dura <rebecca.dura@gmail.com>
Sent:                           Monday, November 9, 2020 12:16 PM
To:                             Maryann Pickering
Subject:                        Site Plan and Plat Amendment - 12197 S. 300 E.



To Whom It May Concern,
I am writing with serious concerns over the proposed site plan and plat amendment at 12197 S. 300 E. I am a
resident homeowner at 446 E. 12000 S. in Draper.

I am concerned about safety issues and traffic issues that would result from a charter school, Beehive
Academy, being built at this location.

There is already a major K-12 school on 300 East: Skaggs Catholic Complex/Juan Diego. The idea of putting
another K-12 school a short distance from Juan Diego on the same street is a ridiculous proposal.

Charter schools, by their very nature, bring in traffic from all over the valley. Charter schools do not offer
buses, so each family has to individually drive their children to school. My children attend a local charter school
(American Preparatory Academy in Draper), and I can attest that I almost never see carpools when I am in line
to pick up my kids from school. As I look across the carpool lanes, I see a single adult in each vehicle, picking
up 1-3 children.

The traffic in this area is already burdensome. We are surrounded with medium-density and high-density
housing on 12300 South and 300 East. The small single family homes on 700 East are being ripped out in favor
of a business corridor. All of this adds additional traffic and congestion-- and unbearable wait times at
intersections.

Pedestrian safety is already at a critical low in this area. We don't have contiguous sidewalks on 12000 South
and 12100 South, among other areas, that allow for pedestrians to walk out of the streets. I have contacted the
Engineering Department several times to request the sidewalks be finished on 12000 South. As a resident of this
street, I witness daily safety issues-- particularly teenagers from Juan Diego driving too fast down 12000 South
to connect to 700 East when school gets out, despite speed bumps and speed limit signs posted.

As a resident of this area, I can also attest to the long wait time at the 300 East/12000 South intersection. It is
very common to wait several minutes and multiple turn signals to be able to turn left onto 12000 South from
300 East. The commercial area currently used by McDonalds and Chick-Fil-A is already overrun by traffic. The
traffic study did not adequately capture the traffic issues in this area. We invite you to come to our
neighborhood in the morning between about 7 am and 8 am, and then again in the afternoon between about 3
pm and 4 pm to view for yourself the traffic situation and the lack of pedestrian safety considerations, such as
sidewalks.

The traffic impact study conducted by Hales Engineering concluded that the 300 East/12300 South and
450 East/12300 South intersections already operate at a failing level of service, prior to adding another K-
12 school on 300 East. How would adding another K-12 charter school possibly be a good idea?

How is it legal to build two K-12 schools almost side by side with a residential neighborhood sandwiched
in between? Is there not legal protection for the residents in the area?

                                                         1
Thank you,
Becky Dura
4467 E. 12000 S.
Draper, UT 84020
Cell: 415-627-7394




                     2
Jeff Hansen
12057 S ​300 E
Draper, UT

November 5, 2020

To Whom it may concern:

We are long-time Draper residents, and close neighbor of the proposed Academy. We already live within
200 yards of the existing Juan Diego Catholic Church. ​The thought of another school even closer
than Juan Diego is unconscionable to us.

A couple of years ago, Richmond America proposed to build 71 single-family homes on the same
10-acre lot that Beehive Academy would like to utilize. I attended the planning commission meeting, and
at the time there were ​almost no detractors​ from the neighborhood. However, the planning commission
recommended denying the 71 single-family homes, citing ​parking concerns​. I can speak for the
neighborhood in saying that ​we would much rather have had 71 single-family homes on this lot than
a 1000-student school​, and the planning committee’s actions of that night are highly questionable.

Regardless, if parking was the reason for denying a single-family neighborhood, the parking situation
stands to be much worse with the proposed Beehive Academy plan.

From Draper City Code 9-25-030:
A.   Parking Required: Every land use established under the authority of this title
shall have parking as required by this chapter. Each person who establishes any such
land use shall provide the required parking. The establishment of a land use shall
include any change in use and any increase in the capacity or intensity of an
existing use.

Hales Engineering’s parking study states that 198 stalls will be “sufficient parking for the anticipated
demand.” From Table 2 of their study, this means that there are only 36 permanent stalls for 110 high
school students, or a ​ratio of 3.1 high-school students per stall; and there are no buses​!

As a comparison, Juan Diego has roughly 800 parking spots for not quite twice the student count, and
even they are daily overflowing onto the side streets and surrounding neighborhoods. There is frequently
a “luge” of cars on 11950 S on both sides of the street, just south of Juan Diego, which is very dangerous
and will become exponentially more so with the increased traffic from the Academy. There are already
many cars permanently parked along 12200/12230 S, and the situation will be even worse with a
1000-student academy nearby.

The proposed parking is ​not​ sufficient for the plan, and the decrease in driving visibility along the nearby
roads will make them much more dangerous than before. ​Beehive Academy needs to either forgo
their 3-acre commercial lot plans (and add more parking), or be denied this plan altogether.

                                              Sincerely,
                                              Jeff Hansen

   EXHIBIT I
PARKING STUDY
                                       MEMORANDUM

Date:          May 5, 2020

To:            Draper City
                                                                            05/05/2020
From:          Hales Engineering


Subject:       Draper Beehive Academy Parking Study
                                                                                           UT20-1665

This memorandum discusses the parking study completed for the proposed Beehive Academy
located in Draper, Utah. The study identifies the Draper City parking rates and parking demand
rates identified by the Institute of Transportation Engineers (ITE).

Project Description

The proposed Beehive Academy project is located on the east side of 300 East, just north of
12200 South in Draper, Utah. A vicinity map of the project site is shown in Figure 1, and a full site
plan is found in Appendix A.




                   Figure 1: Site vicinity map of the project in Draper, Utah

                       1220 North 500 West, Ste. 202 Lehi, UT 84043   p 801.766.4343               1
                                       www.halesengineering.com
The Beehive Academy is planned to have a capacity of 1,000 students in grades K-12. It is
anticipated that the school will have approximately 110 high school students and 890 students in
lower grades. Based on historical Beehive Academy enrollment data, it was assumed that there
will be approximately 210 middle school students and 680 elementary school students.

The current site plan shows 311 parking stalls on site. 113 of the parking stalls on the east side
of the site are planned as evening and event stalls, as they are planned to be used for stacking
and queueing space during drop-off and pick-up times and unavailable for parking use for short
periods of time in the morning and afternoon. This leaves 198 parking stalls that will available all
of the time, with the remaining 113 parking stalls available for most of the day.

Draper City Parking Code

The Draper City code specifies parking rates for various land use types. According to Section 9-
25-100 of the code, the City requires 1 parking stall per 1.25 high school students and 1 parking
stall per 4 elementary and middle school students. The required parking stalls based on the
proposed student count is provided in Table 1. As shown, 311 parking stalls are required based
on City code. The provided 311 parking stalls meet this requirement, though 113 stalls are
planned to be unavailable for approximately 2 hours per weekday.

                          Table 1: Draper City Parking Requirements
                                   Draper City Parking Requirements
                                                             Parking Rate      Required
                        Land Use               Students
                                                           (students/stall)     Parking
                   Elementary School              680             4               170
                     Middle School                210             4               53
                      High School                 110            1.25             88
                         Total                   1,000                            311

ITE Parking Demand Rates

Hales Engineering referenced the ITE Parking Generation, 5th Edition, 2019, to identify national
standards for parking for schools. The ITE Parking Generation rates are based on actual parking
demand data collection at other schools around the country. Based on standard practice, Hales
Engineering referenced the 85th percentile rates for elementary, middle, and high schools. The
85th percentile rates represent a higher-than-average parking demand. The number of stalls
recommended based on ITE parking demand rates is shown in Table 2.

As shown, ITE suggests that approximately 198 parking stalls are needed on site to accommodate
the anticipated demand. With 198 permanent parking stalls on site, this indicates that there is
sufficient parking at all times for the anticipated demand, even during drop-off and pick-up times.




                       1220 North 500 West, Ste. 202 Lehi, UT 84043   p 801.766.4343              2
                                       www.halesengineering.com
                                Table 2: ITE Parking Generation
                                      ITE Parking Generation
                                           Parking Rate - ITE       Equivalent
                                                                                       Required
         Land Use (ITE #)      Students     85th Percentile        Parking Rate
                                                                                        Parking
                                            (stalls/student)     (students/stall)
     Elementary School (520)      680             0.2                   5.0              136
       Middle School (522)        210             0.12                  8.3              26
        High School (530)         110             0.32                  3.1              36
              Total              1,000                                                   198

Discussion

With a total of 311 provided parking stalls, the proposed Beehive Academy meets Draper City’s
required amount of 311 stalls. However, 113 of the stalls will be unavailable for approximately 2
hours per weekday to provide sufficient on-site stacking/queueing space. The parking demand
rates provided by ITE suggest that only 198 stalls are needed for Beehive Academy. This
suggests that the 198 permanent parking stalls will be sufficient for the school. The additional
stalls in the stacking area can be made available during school time between drop-off and pick-
up times as surplus parking supply. Therefore, it is anticipated that the provided parking supply
shown in the site plan is sufficient for Beehive Academy.

Conclusions / Recommendations

The following are the key findings and recommendations from this parking study:
   • The proposed Beehive Academy is anticipated to have a capacity of 1,000 K-12 students,
        with approximately 110 high school students, 210 middle school students, and 680
        elementary school students, based on historical Beehive Academy enrollment data.
   • The current site plan has 311 total planned parking stalls, 198 of which are always
        available. The remaining 113 parking stalls will be unavailable during key drop-off and
        pick-up times for the school, as they will be used for stacking / queueing space.
            o Draper City code requires that 311 parking stalls be provided on site. The site
               meets this requirement, though 113 parking stalls are used during key drop-off and
               pick-up times.
            o According to ITE parking demand rates, it is anticipated that the site will need
               approximately 198 parking stalls for the anticipated demand.
                    Based on the ITE data, it is anticipated that the proposed 311 stalls, and
                       198 permanent stalls, will be sufficient parking for the anticipated demand
                       of the Beehive Academy.

                                       www.halesengineering.com

TRAFFIC IMPACT STUDY
Beehive Academy
Traffic Impact Study Update




             Draper, Utah
                  August 12, 2020
                        UT20-1665
                                                                08/12/2020




1220 North 500 West, Ste. 202 Lehi, UT 84043   p 801.766.4343
                  www.halesengineering.com
                                                            Beehive Academy Charter School
                                                                                      Traffic Impact Study



                                EXECUTIVE SUMMARY

This study addresses the traffic impacts associated with the proposed Beehive Academy Charter
School development located in Draper, Utah. The Beehive Academy Charter School project is
located at approximately 325 East 12200 South.

Included within the analyses for this study are the traffic operations and recommended mitigation
measures for existing conditions and plus project conditions (conditions after development of the
proposed project) at key intersections and roadways near the site. Future 2025 conditions were
also analyzed.

The morning peak hour level of service (LOS) was computed for each study intersection. The
results of this analysis are shown in Table ES-1. Recommended storage lengths are shown in
Table ES-2.




                                                                                                i
                                                            Beehive Academy Charter School
                                                                                      Traffic Impact Study




SUMMARY OF KEY FINDINGS/RECOMMENDATIONS

The following is a summary of key findings and recommendations:
        • The 300 East / 12300 South and 450 East / 12300 South intersections operate at
           failing levels of service during the morning peak hour in existing (2020) background
           conditions. All other study intersections are currently operating at an acceptable LOS
           during the morning peak hour in existing (2020) background conditions.
                o Recommendation: That northbound (to westbound) dual left-turn lanes be
                    implemented at the 300 East / 12300 South intersection.
                o A previous UDOT study of the 450 East / 12300 South intersection found that
                    a signal was warranted; however, the signal has not yet been constructed and
                    has been placed on the Warranted Hold list until Draper can widen 450 East.
        • The development will consist of a charter school (K-12) with a maximum of 1,000
           students.
        • The 300 East / 12300 South, Retail Access / 300 East, and 12200 South / 700 East
           intersections are anticipated to operate at LOS E during the morning peak hour with
           project traffic added. All other study intersections are anticipated to operate at an
           acceptable LOS during the morning peak hour with project traffic added.
                o Recommendation: That additional storage length be added for the eastbound
                    left-turn and southbound right-turn movements at the 300 East / 12300 South
                    intersection.
                o Recommendation: That an eastbound right-turn pocket be added at the 12200
                    South / 700 East intersection.
                o It is anticipated that traffic will reroute from the Retail Access / 300 East
                    intersection to avoid delays.
        • The 300 East / 12300 South intersection is anticipated to operate at LOS E during the
           morning peak hour in future (2025) background conditions. All other study
           intersections are anticipated to operate at an acceptable LOS during the morning peak
           hour in future (2025) background conditions.
                o Recommendation: That a 7-lane cross-section be implemented on 12300
                    South from I-15 to 700 East. This is part of a future project outlined in the
                    Draper City Master Transportation Plan (December 2019).


                                                                                               ii
                                                       Beehive Academy Charter School
                                                                                  Traffic Impact Study



•   All study intersections are anticipated to operate at an acceptable LOS during the
    morning peak hour in future (2025) plus project conditions.
•   All study intersections are also anticipated to operate at an acceptable LOS during the
    afternoon peak hour in future (2025) background conditions.
•   The 300 East / 12300 South and Access 3 / 300 East intersections are anticipated to
    operate at failing levels of service during the afternoon peak hour in future (2025) plus
    project conditions.
         o Recommendation: That dual southbound (to eastbound) left-turns be added to
            the 300 East / 12300 South intersection. This will reduce queuing and allow for
            an appropriate level of service at the Access 3 / 300 East intersection.
•   An on-site out-load queuing analysis was conducted for Beehive Academy. It was
    determined that a queuing length of at least 2,250 feet be implemented to maintain
    adequate traffic flow during afternoon pick-up. Based on existing site plans, which
    indicate five stacking lanes of 500 feet plus an additional 100 feet of space between
    stacking lanes and the pick-up area (totaling 2,600 feet), these requirements are being
    met by Beehive Academy.




                                                                                           iii
                                                                                                      Beehive Academy Charter School
                                                                                                                                                  Traffic Impact Study
